16	
17	# LaTeX 论文写作全流程
18	
19	ML/AI 顶会论文从草稿到投稿的完整指南。整合 Orchestra Research、VibeSkills、claim2evidence 三方最佳实践。
20	
21	---
22	
23	## 1. 工作模式
24	
25	开始前选择模式：
26	
27	| 模式 | 说明 | 适用 |
28	|------|------|------|
29	| **Build** | 从实验笔记/结果/代码生成论文 | 新稿 |
30	| **Improve** | 改写已有章节，强化论证 | 修改稿 |
31	| **Audit** | 输出风险列表 + 缺失证据 + 修复建议 | 投前检查 |
32	
33	---
34	
35	## 2. Claim-Evidence 方法论
36	
37	**核心原则：论文是一个有中心贡献的故事，不是实验的堆砌。**
38	
39	### 2.1 一句话贡献
40	
41	在写任何内容之前，必须能用一句话说清贡献。如果不能，说明还没准备好写论文。
42	
43	### 2.2 Claim-Evidence Map
44	
45	为每个 contribution 建立 claim→evidence 映射表：
46	
47	| Contribution | Claim | Evidence | Table/Figure |
48	|-------------|-------|----------|-------------|
49	| C1: 方法 X 在 Y 上 SOTA | 比所有 baseline 高 ≥2% | 5-seed 平均，p<0.05 | Table 1 |
50	| C2: 消融证明 Z 是关键 | 去掉 Z 后掉 >5% | 消融实验 | Table 2 |
51	| C3: 计算效率 | 比 baseline 快 3× | FLOPs + wall-clock | Figure 3 |
52	
53	**没有直接证据支撑的 claim 必须删除或降级为 discussion。**
54	
55	### 2.3 章节起草顺序
56	
57	```
58	1. Introduction + Experiments（先确定 claim 和 evidence）
59	2. Method（评估问题稳定后再写细节）
60	3. Related Work
61	4. Abstract + Title（核心 claim 定型后最后写）
62	```
63	
64	### 2.4 Quality Gates
65	
66	投稿前必须通过：
67	- [ ] 前两段出现显式 novelty 声明
68	- [ ] 每个 contribution 有可验证的 evidence
69	- [ ] Abstract / Intro / Conclusion 的 claim 一致
70	- [ ] Limitations 真实且技术性诚实
71	- [ ] 审稿人两分钟内能识别 delta
72	
73	---
74	
75	## 3. 引用防幻觉（CRITICAL）
76	
77	**AI 生成引用有 ~40% 错误率。绝对禁止凭记忆写 BibTeX。**
78	
79	### 3.1 黄金法则
80	
81	```
82	IF 无法程序化获取引用:
83	    → 标记 [CITATION NEEDED] 或 \cite{PLACEHOLDER_verify}
84	    → 明确告知用户
85	    → 绝不编造看似合理的引用
86	```
87	
88	### 3.2 五步验证流程
89	
90	```
91	1. SEARCH  → Semantic Scholar API 搜索关键词
92	2. VERIFY  → 至少 2 个来源确认论文存在（S2 + arXiv/CrossRef）
93	3. RETRIEVE → 通过 DOI 获取 BibTeX（程序化，非记忆）
94	4. CHECK   → 确认你引用的 claim 确实出自该论文
95	5. ADD     → 加入 .bib 文件
96	```
97	
98	### 3.3 API 速查
99	
100	| API | 用途 | 限制 |
101	|-----|------|------|
102	| **Semantic Scholar** | ML/AI 论文搜索 | 1 RPS (免费) |
103	| **CrossRef** | DOI → BibTeX | 需 mailto 头 |
104	| **arXiv** | 预印本搜索 | 3s 间隔 |
105	
106	```python
107	# DOI → BibTeX（最可靠的方式）
108	import requests
109	def doi_to_bibtex(doi: str) -> str:
110	    r = requests.get(f"https://doi.org/{doi}",
111	                     headers={"Accept": "application/x-bibtex"})
112	    r.raise_for_status()
113	    return r.text
114	
115	# Semantic Scholar 搜索
116	from semanticscholar import SemanticScholar
117	sch = SemanticScholar()
118	results = sch.search_paper("attention mechanism transformers", limit=5)
119	for p in results:
120	    print(f"{p.title} | DOI: {p.externalIds.get('DOI', 'N/A')}")
````

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We evaluate stagnation detection across four axes: in-domain accuracy, cross-agent transfer, robustness to design choices, and temporal dynamics of labeling functions.
5	All reported metrics are evaluated against silver labels derived from majority vote over three labeling functions (\S\ref{sec:method}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Circular evaluation caveat.}
8	Structural features (command repetition ratios, file operation counts, edit distances) are computed from the same signals that the labeling functions use to produce silver labels.
9	Consequently, the in-domain structural F1 of 0.9951 reflects an upper bound on how well these features can \emph{reverse-engineer} the labeling functions, not detection accuracy against true ground truth.
10	The cross-agent evaluation on OpenHands (0.967 macro-F1) is non-circular: models trained on SWE-Agent LF distributions are tested on a different agent whose LF firing patterns differ substantially (\S\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).
11	A human-annotated gold set provides the definitive non-circular evaluation (\S\ref{sec:gold_set}).
12	
13	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
14	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
15	\label{sec:setup}
16	
17	\paragraph{Datasets.}
18	We extract sliding windows of $k{=}5$ consecutive actions from agent trajectories.
19	\textbf{SWE-Agent} trajectories from SWE-bench \citep{jimenez2024swebench,yang2024sweagent} yield 54,809 windows with a stagnation ratio of 28.5\%.
20	\textbf{OpenHands} (OH) trajectories \citep{wang2025openhands} yield 150,652 windows with a stagnation ratio of 6.6\%.
21	Silver labels are produced by majority vote over three labeling functions; confidence equals the number of agreeing LFs (1--3).
22	Unless stated otherwise, evaluation uses the conf${\geq}$2 subset: $N{=}1{,}824$ for SWE-Agent (485 stagnation, 1,339 progress) and $N{=}43{,}376$ for OH.
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\small
27	\begin{tabular}{llrrc}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Agent} & \textbf{Source} & \textbf{Windows} & \textbf{Stag\,\%} & \textbf{Conf${\geq}$2} \\
30	\midrule
31	SWE-Agent & SWE-bench & 54,809 & 28.5 & 15,401 \\
32	OpenHands & CodeAct   & 150,652 & 6.6 & 43,376 \\
33	\bottomrule
34	\end{tabular}
35	\caption{Dataset statistics. Stagnation ratios differ substantially across agents, reflecting different action distributions.}
36	\label{tab:dataset_stats}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	\paragraph{Detection methods.}
40	(1)~\textbf{Structural features}: 30 hand-crafted features (repetition rates, file operation counts, edit distances, output entropy) fed to Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).
41	(2)~\textbf{ProgressProbe}: Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} embeddings (1024-dim) processed by a 2-layer GRU \citep{cho2014gru} with attention \citep{bahdanau2015attention} (hidden$=$256, dropout$=$0.3), early-stopped at epoch~3 with patience~5.
42	(3)~\textbf{Mean-pool + LR}: Mean-pooled Qwen3 embeddings with logistic regression.
43	(4)~\textbf{LLM-as-Judge}: Claude Sonnet zero-shot window-level classification (\$11.66 API cost for the SWE test set).
44	(5)~\textbf{Cosine similarity}: Cosine distance between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.
45	
46	\paragraph{Metrics.}
47	Macro-F1 is the primary metric. We also report per-class F1 for stagnation (F1\textsubscript{stag}) and progress (F1\textsubscript{prog}).
48	
49	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
50	\subsection{Main Results}
51	\label{sec:main_results}
52	
53	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} presents in-domain results on the SWE-Agent conf${\geq}$2 test set and cross-agent transfer to OpenHands.
54	
55	\begin{table*}[t]
56	\centering
57	\small
58	\begin{tabular}{l ccc cc}
59	\toprule
60	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{SWE-Agent In-Domain} (conf${\geq}$2)} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{OpenHands Transfer}} \\
61	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6}
62	\textbf{Method} & Macro-F1 & F1\textsubscript{stag} & F1\textsubscript{prog} & All & Conf${\geq}$2 \\
63	\midrule
64	Structural RF$^\dagger$       & \textbf{0.9951} & 0.9928 & 0.9974 & 0.9123 & \textbf{0.9959} \\
65	Structural GB$^\dagger$       & \textbf{0.9951} & 0.9928 & 0.9974 & \textbf{0.9674} & \underline{0.9919} \\
66	Structural LR$^\dagger$       & 0.9874 & 0.9815 & 0.9933 & 0.7801 & 0.9671 \\
67	ProgressProbe (GRU)           & \underline{0.9055} & 0.8593 & 0.9518 & 0.5280 & --- \\
68	\quad + full OH fine-tune      & ---    & ---    & ---    & 0.7510 & --- \\
69	Mean-pool + LR                & 0.8435 & 0.7620 & 0.9250 & ---    & --- \\
70	LLM-as-Judge                  & 0.6625 & 0.6055 & 0.7195 & ---    & --- \\
71	Cosine Similarity             & 0.5606 & ---    & ---    & ---    & --- \\
72	\bottomrule
73	\end{tabular}
74	\caption{Stagnation detection results. In-domain evaluation on SWE-Agent conf${\geq}$2 test set ($N{=}1{,}824$) and zero-shot cross-agent transfer to OpenHands. $^\dagger$Structural metrics are evaluated against LF-derived labels and reflect LF-reconstructibility rather than ground-truth accuracy (see circular evaluation caveat). Best in \textbf{bold}, second-best \underline{underlined}.}
75	\label{tab:main_results}
76	\end{table*}
77	
78	In-domain, structural RF achieves 0.9951 macro-F1, exceeding the GRU probe by 8.96 percentage points (pp) and the LLM judge by 24.3\,pp.
79	However, the structural--probe gap is inflated by circular evaluation: structural features reconstruct the LFs that generated the labels.
80	The more informative comparison is among non-circular methods: the GRU probe outperforms mean-pool~+~LR by 6.2\,pp, confirming that temporal modeling within windows adds value beyond static embedding aggregation.
81	The LLM judge underperforms all learned methods despite substantially higher cost.
82	
83	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
84	\subsection{Cross-Agent Transfer}
85	\label{sec:cross_agent}
86	
87	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} (right columns) shows models trained on SWE-Agent and evaluated on OpenHands without any OH training data.
88	Structural GB transfers best, achieving 0.967 macro-F1 on all OH windows---21.6\,pp above the probe fine-tuned on 100\% of OH data (0.751).
89	RF drops to 0.912 on all-confidence OH data despite matching GB in-domain, suggesting GB's boosted ensemble is more robust to distributional shift.
90	Both structural models recover to $>$0.99 on conf${\geq}$2 OH windows, where label noise is lower.
91	
92	The probe's zero-shot transfer (0.528) is near chance, and even full OH fine-tuning only reaches 0.751.
93	This asymmetry---structural features transfer naturally while learned embeddings do not---is a central empirical finding: stagnation manifests in action-level structure that generalizes across agents.
94	
95	\paragraph{Multi-seed stability.}
96	Structural RF: $0.9951{\pm}0.0000$ in-domain (5 seeds, zero variance); GB cross-agent: $0.9678{\pm}0.0015$.
97	The probe shows higher variance: $0.9185{\pm}0.0113$, though all seeds exceed 0.90.
98	
99	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
100	\subsection{Robustness Analysis}
101	\label{sec:robustness}
102	
103	\paragraph{Encoder ablation.}
104	Replacing Qwen3-0.6B with BGE-M3 \citep{chen2024bgem3} or E5-large-v2 \citep{wang2024e5} yields probe macro-F1 of 0.9413 and 0.9363, respectively (vs.\ 0.9055 for Qwen3).
105	All encoders exceed 0.90, indicating the probe's temporal architecture, not a specific encoder, drives performance. Full results appear in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:encoder_ablation}.
106	
107	\paragraph{Window size.}
108	$k{=}5$ is optimal (0.9309 macro-F1); $k{=}3$ drops 5.9\,pp and $k{=}7$ drops 1.8\,pp.
109	Shorter windows lack sufficient context; longer windows dilute signal by spanning both progress and stagnation phases.
110	Details in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:window_ablation}.
111	
112	\paragraph{Data scaling.}
113	Figure~\ref{fig:data_scaling} shows probe performance scales monotonically from 0.8054 (10\% training data) to 0.9055 (100\%), with structural RF providing a ceiling at 0.9951 (dashed line).
114	The curve has not plateaued, suggesting larger trajectory corpora could further close the structural--probe gap.
115	
116	\begin{figure}[t]
117	\centering
118	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_data_scaling.pdf}
119	\caption{Probe macro-F1 vs.\ training data fraction. Structural RF ceiling shown as dashed line. Performance scales monotonically but has not saturated at 100\% data.}
120	\label{fig:data_scaling}
121	\end{figure}
122	
123	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
124	\subsection{Labeling Function Temporal Dynamics}
125	\label{sec:lf_temporal}
126	
127	We analyze when each LF first fires relative to the onset of stagnation episodes (Table~\ref{tab:lf_temporal}, Figure~\ref{fig:lf_temporal}).
128	
129	\begin{table}[t]
130	\centering
131	\small
132	\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
133	\toprule
134	\textbf{LF} & \textbf{Lag} & \textbf{Lead\,\%} & \textbf{Conc.\,\%} & \textbf{Lag\,\%} \\
135	\midrule
136	cmd\_rep      & $-1.87$ & 36.1 & --- & --- \\
137	file\_diff    & $-1.20$ & 17.1 & --- & --- \\
138	outcome\_bp   & $+6.70$ & ---  & --- & 48.4 \\
139	\bottomrule
140	\end{tabular}
141	\caption{Temporal roles of labeling functions on SWE-Agent. Lag = mean steps relative to stagnation onset (negative = leading). Kruskal-Wallis $H{=}274.3$, $p{=}2.74{\times}10^{-60}$; all pairwise comparisons significant after Bonferroni correction.}
142	\label{tab:lf_temporal}
143	\end{table}
144	
145	The three LFs occupy distinct temporal niches.
146	Command repetition leads stagnation onset by 1.87 steps on average, making it an early warning signal.
147	File diff is near-concurrent ($-1.20$ steps).
148	Outcome backpropagation lags by 6.70 steps, acting as a retrospective confirmation.
149	
150	All pairwise differences are significant (Kruskal-Wallis $H{=}274.3$, $p{=}2.74{\times}10^{-60}$; Bonferroni-corrected pairwise: cmd\_rep vs.\ file\_diff $p{=}1.44{\times}10^{-26}$, cmd\_rep vs.\ outcome\_bp $p{=}6.67{\times}10^{-39}$, file\_diff vs.\ outcome\_bp $p{=}3.47{\times}10^{-47}$).
151	
152	\paragraph{Cross-agent reversal.}
153	On OpenHands, cmd\_rep shifts from leading ($-1.87$) to lagging ($+3.32$ steps).
154	This reversal explains the cmd\_rep pathology analyzed in \S\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}: OH's action distribution triggers cmd\_rep on progress windows, inverting its diagnostic value.
155	
156	\begin{figure}[t]
157	\centering
158	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_lf_temporal.pdf}
159	\caption{Temporal roles of labeling functions relative to stagnation onset on SWE-Agent. cmd\_rep leads ($-1.87$ steps), file\_diff is near-concurrent ($-1.20$), outcome\_bp lags ($+6.70$). Inset: OH reversal where cmd\_rep becomes lagging.}
160	\label{fig:lf_temporal}
161	\end{figure}
162	
163	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
164	\subsection{Command Repetition Ablation}
165	\label{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}
166	
167	The cmd\_rep LF fires on 92.3\% of OH windows (vs.\ 65.6\% on SWE-Agent), with a 71\% false positive rate on OH progress windows.
168	Table~\ref{tab:cmd_rep_ablation} shows the effect of removing it.
169	
170	\begin{table}[t]
171	\centering
172	\small
173	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
174	\toprule
175	\textbf{LF Config} & \textbf{ZS F1} & \textbf{FT F1} & \textbf{FT F1\textsubscript{s}} & \textbf{FT F1\textsubscript{p}} \\
176	\midrule
177	3-LF (w/ cmd\_rep) & 0.530 & 0.751 & 0.544 & 0.959 \\
178	2-LF (no cmd\_rep) & 0.607 & \textbf{0.927} & 0.938 & 0.917 \\
179	\midrule
180	$\Delta$ & +7.7\,pp & +17.6\,pp & +39.4\,pp & $-4.2$\,pp \\
181	\bottomrule
182	\end{tabular}
183	\caption{Effect of removing cmd\_rep on OpenHands probe performance. ZS = zero-shot transfer from SWE-Agent; FT = fine-tuned on 100\% OH data. F1\textsubscript{s}/F1\textsubscript{p} = stagnation/progress F1.}
184	\label{tab:cmd_rep_ablation}
185	\end{table}
186	
187	Removing cmd\_rep improves zero-shot transfer by 7.7\,pp and fine-tuned performance by 17.6\,pp.
188	The gain is driven almost entirely by stagnation recall (+39.4\,pp F1\textsubscript{stag}), at a modest cost to progress detection ($-4.2$\,pp F1\textsubscript{prog}).
189	The 3-LF fine-tuned model's stagnation F1 of 0.544 reveals that cmd\_rep corrupts silver labels so thoroughly that even full fine-tuning on OH data cannot recover.
190	This motivates agent-specific LF selection: labeling functions validated on one agent architecture may become adversarial on another.
191	
192	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
193	\subsection{Temporal Label Models}
194	\label{sec:temporal_label_models}
195	
196	We investigate whether incorporating LF temporal dynamics into label aggregation improves over majority vote (MV).
197	Table~\ref{tab:temporal_models} compares three temporal label models \citep{ratner2017snorkel,safranchik2020linkedhmm}.
198	
199	\begin{table}[t]
200	\centering
201	\small
202	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
203	\toprule
204	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Conf${\geq}$2 F1} & \textbf{MV Agr.} & \textbf{Probe F1$^\ddagger$} \\
205	\midrule
206	MV (baseline) & 0.8946$^\ast$ & 1.000 & 0.8468 \\
207	TWV ($\sigma{=}2$) & \textbf{0.9003} & 0.885 & 0.8211 \\
208	Lag-HMM & 0.8936 & 0.820 & --- \\
209	HMM & 0.7502 & 0.685 & 0.6564 \\
210	\bottomrule
211	\end{tabular}
212	\caption{Temporal label model comparison. $^\ast$MV conf${\geq}$2 F1 is a self-evaluation baseline (MV evaluated against itself on high-confidence windows). $^\ddagger$Downstream: probe trained on each method's labels, evaluated on SWE-Agent conf${\geq}$2 vs.\ MV labels.}
213	\label{tab:temporal_models}
214	\end{table}
215	
216	TWV achieves the highest conf${\geq}$2 F1 (0.9003, +0.57\,pp over MV), but this gain does not propagate downstream: a probe trained on TWV labels scores 0.8211 vs.\ 0.8468 for MV-trained ($-2.57$\,pp).
217	TWV flips 14.1\% of conf$=$1 labels, introducing noise that hurts probe training.
218	HMM overpredicts stagnation (29,176 stagnation vs.\ 25,633 progress windows), degrading downstream performance to 0.6564.
219	Lag-HMM closely tracks MV ($-0.1$\,pp) despite explicitly modeling temporal offsets, suggesting that the temporal structure captured by individual LFs does not improve \emph{aggregation}.
220	
221	\paragraph{TWV sensitivity.}
222	The Gaussian kernel bandwidth $\sigma$ is critical: $\sigma{=}2$ is optimal (0.9003); $\sigma{=}1$ drops to 0.8105 and $\sigma{\geq}5$ falls below 0.81.
223	This narrow operating range, combined with the downstream degradation, makes TWV impractical as a drop-in MV replacement.
224	
225	These results constitute a negative finding: despite clear temporal structure in LF firing patterns (\S\ref{sec:lf_temporal}), temporal label models do not improve over simple majority vote for downstream stagnation detection.
226	
227	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
228	\subsection{Gold Set Validation}
229	\label{sec:gold_set}
230	\goldset{Human-annotated gold set (650 windows) validation pending. Results will be added upon completion (expected 2026-05-17). This provides the non-circular ground truth evaluation for all methods.}

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe evaluate stagnation detection across four axes: in-domain accuracy, cross-agent transfer, robustness to design choices, and temporal dynamics of labeling functions.\nAll reported metrics are evaluated against silver labels derived from majority vote over three labeling functions (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n\n\\paragraph{Circular evaluation caveat.}\nStructural features (command repetition ratios, file operation counts, edit distances) are computed from the same signals that the labeling functions use to produce silver labels.\nConsequently, the in-domain structural F1 of 0.9951 reflects an upper bound on how well these features can \\emph{reverse-engineer} the labeling functions, not detection accuracy against true ground truth.\nThe cross-agent evaluation on OpenHands (0.967 macro-F1) is non-circular: models trained on SWE-Agent LF distributions are tested on a different agent whose LF firing patterns differ substantially (\\S\\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).\nA human-annotated gold set provides the definitive non-circular evaluation (\\S\\ref{sec:gold_set}).\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe extract sliding windows of $k{=}5$ consecutive actions from agent trajectories.\n\\textbf{SWE-Agent} trajectories from SWE-bench \\citep{jimenez2024swebench,yang2024sweagent} yield 54,809 windows with a stagnation ratio of 28.5\\%.\n\\textbf{OpenHands} (OH) trajectories \\citep{wang2025openhands} yield 150,652 windows with a stagnation ratio of 6.6\\%.\nSilver labels are produced by majority vote over three labeling functions; confidence equals the number of agreeing LFs (1--3).\nUnless stated otherwise, evaluation uses the conf${\\geq}$2 subset: $N{=}1{,}824$ for SWE-Agent (485 stagnation, 1,339 progress) and $N{=}43{,}376$ for OH.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Agent} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Windows} & \\textbf{Stag\\,\\%} & \\textbf{Conf${\\geq}$2} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSWE-Agent & SWE-bench & 54,809 & 28.5 & 15,401 \\\\\nOpenHands & CodeAct   & 150,652 & 6.6 & 43,376 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. Stagnation ratios differ substantially across agents, reflecting different action distributions.}\n\\label{tab:dataset_stats}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Detection methods.}\n(1)~\\textbf{Structural features}: 30 hand-crafted features (repetition rates, file operation counts, edit distances, output entropy) fed to Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).\n(2)~\\textbf{ProgressProbe}: Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \\citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} embeddings (1024-dim) processed by a 2-layer GRU \\citep{cho2014gru} with attention \\citep{bahdanau2015attention} (hidden$=$256, dropout$=$0.3), early-stopped at epoch~3 with patience~5.\n(3)~\\textbf{Mean-pool + LR}: Mean-pooled Qwen3 embeddings with logistic regression.\n(4)~\\textbf{LLM-as-Judge}: Claude Sonnet zero-shot window-level classification (\\$11.66 API cost for the SWE test set).\n(5)~\\textbf{Cosine similarity}: Cosine distance between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics.}\nMacro-F1 is the primary metric. We also report per-class F1 for stagnation (F1\\textsubscript{stag}) and progress (F1\\textsubscript{prog}).\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} presents in-domain results on the SWE-Agent conf${\\geq}$2 test set and cross-agent transfer to OpenHands.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc cc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{SWE-Agent In-Domain} (conf${\\geq}$2)} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{OpenHands Transfer}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6}\n\\textbf{Method} & Macro-F1 & F1\\textsubscript{stag} & F1\\textsubscript{prog} & All & Conf${\\geq}$2 \\\\\n\\midrule\nStructural RF$^\\dagger$       & \\textbf{0.9951} & 0.9928 & 0.9974 & 0.9123 & \\textbf{0.9959} \\\\\nStructural GB$^\\dagger$       & \\textbf{0.9951} & 0.9928 & 0.9974 & \\textbf{0.9674} & \\underline{0.9919} \\\\\nStructural LR$^\\dagger$       & 0.9874 & 0.9815 & 0.9933 & 0.7801 & 0.9671 \\\\\nProgressProbe (GRU)           & \\underline{0.9055} & 0.8593 & 0.9518 & 0.5280 & --- \\\\\n\\quad + full OH fine-tune      & ---    & ---    & ---    & 0.7510 & --- \\\\\nMean-pool + LR                & 0.8435 & 0.7620 & 0.9250 & ---    & --- \\\\\nLLM-as-Judge                  & 0.6625 & 0.6055 & 0.7195 & ---    & --- \\\\\nCosine Similarity             & 0.5606 & ---    & ---    & ---    & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Stagnation detection results. temporal dynamics into label aggregation improves over majority vote (MV).\nTable~\\ref{tab:temporal_models} compares three temporal label models \\citep{ratner2017snorkel,safranchik2020linkedhmm}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Conf${\\geq}$2 F1} & \\textbf{MV Agr.} & \\textbf{Probe F1$^\\ddagger$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMV (baseline) & 0.8946$^\\ast$ & 1.000 & 0.8468 \\\\\nTWV ($\\sigma{=}2$) & \\textbf{0.9003} & 0.885 & 0.8211 \\\\\nLag-HMM & 0.8936 & 0.820 & --- \\\\\nHMM & 0.7502 & 0.685 & 0.6564 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Temporal label model comparison. $^\\ast$MV conf${\\geq}$2 F1 is a self-evaluation baseline (MV evaluated against itself on high-confidence windows). $^\\ddagger$Downstream: probe trained on each method's labels, evaluated on SWE-Agent conf${\\geq}$2 vs.\\ MV labels.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_models}\n\\end{table}\n\nTWV achieves the highest conf${\\geq}$2 F1 (0.9003, +0.57\\,pp over MV), but this gain does not propagate downstream: a probe trained on TWV labels scores 0.8211 vs.\\ 0.8468 for MV-trained ($-2.57$\\,pp).\nTWV flips 14.1\\% of conf$=$1 labels, introducing noise that hurts probe training.\nHMM overpredicts stagnation (29,176 stagnation vs.\\ 25,633 progress windows), degrading downstream performance to 0.6564.\nLag-HMM closely tracks MV ($-0.1$\\,pp) despite explicitly modeling temporal offsets, suggesting that the temporal structure captured by individual LFs does not improve \\emph{aggregation}.\n\n\\paragraph{TWV sensitivity.}\nThe Gaussian kernel bandwidth $\\sigma$ is critical: $\\sigma{=}2$ is optimal (0.9003); $\\sigma{=}1$ drops to 0.8105 and $\\sigma{\\geq}5$ falls below 0.81.\nThis narrow operating range, combined with the downstream degradation, makes TWV impractical as a drop-in MV replacement.\n\nThese results constitute a negative finding: despite clear temporal structure in LF firing patterns (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}), temporal label models do not improve over simple majority vote for downstream stagnation detection.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Gold Set Validation}\n\\label{sec:gold_set}\n\\goldset{Human-annotated gold set (650 windows) validation pending. Results will be added upon completion (expected 2026-05-17). This provides the non-circular ground truth evaluation for all methods.}",
      "numLines": 230,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 230
    }
  }
}

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513/projects/agent_progress_probe/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe frame stagnation detection as window-level binary classification over agent trajectories.\nSilver labels are generated via weak supervision from three labeling functions, then used to train and evaluate three detection approaches: structural features, a neural embedding probe, and an LLM-as-judge baseline.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} provides a system overview.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{System overview. (Left) Agent trajectories are segmented into overlapping windows of $k{=}5$ steps. (Center) Three labeling functions vote on each window; majority vote produces silver labels with confidence 1--3. (Right) Three detection approaches compared: structural features (30-dim), NL embedding probe (GRU), and LLM-as-judge (eval only, not trained on silver labels).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:problem}\n\nGiven an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_n)$, we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \\ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$.\nThe task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$, where 0 denotes \\emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \\emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway).\nWindow-level classification captures temporal dynamics that step-level scoring misses, while offering finer granularity than trajectory-level labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Weak Supervision Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:weak_supervision}\n\nNo gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nWe adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.\n\n\\subsubsection{Labeling Functions}\n\\label{sec:labeling_functions}\n\nWe design three LFs that target complementary signals of stagnation (Table~\\ref{tab:lf_design}).\n\n\\paragraph{Command repetition ($\\lambda_1$).}\nComputes the proportion of repeated commands within window $w_i$.\nA high repetition ratio triggers a stagnation vote, capturing overt repetitive behavior such as re-running failing commands.\nThis LF fires on 65.6\\% of SWE-Agent windows and 92.3\\% of OpenHands windows; the elevated OH rate reflects differences in action distributions across agent architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{File diff delta ($\\lambda_2$).}\nMeasures the fraction of steps within $w_i$ that produce code modifications (file creates, edits, or deletes).\nA low modification frequency triggers a stagnation vote, detecting lack of productive output.\nOn OpenHands, this LF achieves 87\\% correctness on progress windows, making it the most reliable cross-agent signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, late-trajectory windows receive stagnation votes under the assumption that failure is preceded by unproductive behavior.\nThis LF provides retrospective confirmation rather than real-time detection.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{LF} & \\textbf{Signal} & \\textbf{SWE Fire\\,\\%} & \\textbf{OH Fire\\,\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\lambda_1$ (cmd\\_rep)    & Repetition & 65.6 & 92.3 \\\\\n$\\lambda_2$ (file\\_diff)  & Output     & ---  & ---  \\\\\n$\\lambda_3$ (outcome\\_bp) & Outcome    & ---  & ---  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Labeling function design. Fire\\,\\% = fraction of windows on which the LF produces a stagnation vote. SWE = SWE-Agent; OH = OpenHands.}\n\\label{tab:lf_design}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsubsection{Label Aggregation}\n\\label{sec:label_aggregation}\n\nSilver labels are produced by majority vote:\n\\begin{equation}\ny_i = \\text{MV}\\bigl(\\lambda_1(w_i),\\; \\lambda_2(w_i),\\; \\lambda_3(w_i)\\bigr)\n\\label{eq:mv}\n\\end{equation}\nWe define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Detection Methods}\n\\label{sec:detection_methods}\n\n\\subsubsection{Structural Features}\n\\label{sec:structural_features}\n\nWe extract a 30-dimensional feature vector from each window, requiring no embedding model.\nFeatures include: exact and fuzzy command repetition ratios, unique command ratio, file operation counts (create, modify, delete), edit distances between consecutive actions, output entropy, and action type distributions.\nThese features are fed to three classifiers: Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).\n\n\\subsubsection{NL Embedding Probe (ProgressProbe)}\n\\label{sec:progressprobe}\n\nEach action $a_t$ is encoded as a natural language description and embedded using Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \\citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} into a 1024-dimensional vector.\nThe $k$ embeddings within a window are processed by a 2-layer GRU \\citep{cho2014gru} with hidden size 256 and dropout 0.3.\nAn attention mechanism \\citep{bahdanau2015attention} pools the GRU outputs into a single window representation, which is passed through a fully connected layer with sigmoid activation for binary classification.\nWe also evaluate two simpler baselines: mean-pooled embeddings with logistic regression, and cosine similarity between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.\n\n\\subsubsection{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}\n\\label{sec:llm_judge}\n\nWe prompt Claude Sonnet in zero-shot mode to classify each window as stagnation or progress, given the textual descriptions of actions within the window.\nThis baseline serves exclusively for evaluation---it is not used in the silver label pipeline and incurs \\$11.66 in API cost for the SWE-Agent test set alone, making it impractical for large-scale labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Temporal Label Models}\n\\label{sec:temporal_models}\n\nThe three LFs occupy distinct temporal niches relative to stagnation onset (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}): $\\lambda_1$ leads by ${\\sim}2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ is near-concurrent, and $\\lambda_3$ lags by ${\\sim}7$ steps.\nThis motivates exploring whether encoding temporal structure into label aggregation can improve over majority vote.\n\n\\subsubsection{Time-Varying Weights (TWV)}\n\\label{sec:twv}\n\nTWV applies Gaussian kernel smoothing to each LF's activation sequence:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\tilde{\\lambda}_j(t) = \\sum_{t'} K_\\sigma(t - t') \\cdot \\lambda_j(t')\n\\label{eq:twv}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $K_\\sigma$ is a Gaussian kernel with bandwidth $\\sigma$.\nSmoothed activations replace raw votes for conf${=}1$ windows (where exactly one LF disagrees), potentially flipping labels in temporally ambiguous regions.\nThe bandwidth $\\sigma$ controls the extent of temporal context: larger values incorporate more distant steps.\n\n\\subsubsection{Hidden Markov Models}\n\\label{sec:hmm}\n\nWe model the label sequence as a Hidden Markov Model \\citep{safranchik2020linkedhmm} with two hidden states (stagnation, progress) and observations defined by the joint activation pattern of the three LFs.\nTransition and emission parameters are estimated via Expectation-Maximization (EM).\n\n\\subsubsection{Lag-Aware HMM}\n\\label{sec:lag_hmm}\n\nThe standard HMM treats all LFs as synchronous.\nLag-Aware HMM extends this by explicitly modeling each LF's temporal offset: $\\lambda_1$ is shifted by $-2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ by $-1$, and $\\lambda_3$ by $+7$, estimated from the temporal dynamics analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}).\nTo prevent divergence, we apply two regularization strategies: (1)~\\emph{confidence anchoring}, which fixes labels for conf${\\geq}$2 windows and restricts EM updates to conf${=}1$ windows; and (2)~a prior strength of 5000 on transition parameters.\nThese constraints keep the model close to the majority vote baseline while allowing it to resolve temporally ambiguous labels.\n"
}

    "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe frame stagnation detection as window-level binary classification over agent trajectories.\nSilver labels are generated via weak supervision from three labeling functions, then used to train and evaluate three detection approaches: structural features, a neural embedding probe, and an LLM-as-judge baseline.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} provides a system overview.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{System overview. (Left) Agent trajectories are segmented into overlapping windows of $k{=}5$ steps. (Center) Three labeling functions vote on each window; majority vote produces silver labels with confidence 1--3. (Right) Three detection approaches compared: structural features (30-dim), NL embedding probe (GRU), and LLM-as-judge (eval only, not trained on silver labels).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:problem}\n\nGiven an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_n)$, we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \\ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$.\nThe task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$, where 0 denotes \\emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \\emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway).\nWindow-level classification captures temporal dynamics that step-level scoring misses, while offering finer granularity than trajectory-level labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Weak Supervision Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:weak_supervision}\n\nNo gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nWe adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.\n\n\\subsubsection{Labeling Functions}\n\\label{sec:labeling_functions}\n\nWe design three LFs that target complementary signals of stagnation (Table~\\ref{tab:lf_design}).\n\n\\paragraph{Command repetition ($\\lambda_1$).}\nComputes the proportion of repeated commands within window $w_i$.\nA high repetition ratio triggers a stagnation vote, capturing overt repetitive behavior such as re-running failing commands.\nThis LF fires on 65.6\\% of SWE-Agent windows and 92.3\\% of OpenHands windows; the elevated OH rate reflects differences in action distributions across agent architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{File diff delta ($\\lambda_2$).}\nMeasures the fraction of steps within $w_i$ that produce code modifications (file creates, edits, or deletes).\nA low modification frequency triggers a stagnation vote, detecting lack of productive output.\nOn OpenHands, this LF achieves 87\\% correctness on progress windows, making it the most reliable cross-agent signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, late-trajectory windows receive stagnation votes under the assumption that failure is preceded by unproductive behavior.\nThis LF provides retrospective confirmation rather than real-time detection.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{LF} & \\textbf{Signal} & \\textbf{SWE Fire\\,\\%} & \\textbf{OH Fire\\,\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\lambda_1$ (cmd\\_rep)    & Repetition & 65.6 & 92.3 \\\\\n$\\lambda_2$ (file\\_diff)  & Output     & ---  & ---  \\\\\n$\\lambda_3$ (outcome\\_bp) & Outcome    & ---  & ---  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Labeling function design. Fire\\,\\% = fraction of windows on which the LF produces a stagnation vote. SWE = SWE-Agent; OH = OpenHands.}\n\\label{tab:lf_design}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsubsection{Label Aggregation}\n\\label{sec:label_aggregation}\n\nSilver labels are produced by majority vote:\n\\begin{equation}\ny_i = \\text{MV}\\bigl(\\lambda_1(w_i),\\; \\lambda_2(w_i),\\; \\lambda_3(w_i)\\bigr)\n\\label{eq:mv}\n\\end{equation}\nWe define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Detection Methods}\n\\label{sec:detection_methods}\n\n\\subsubsection{Structural Features}\n\\label{sec:structural_features}\n\nWe extract a 30-dimensional feature vector from each window, requiring no embedding model.\nFeatures include: exact and fuzzy command repetition ratios, unique command ratio, file operation counts (create, modify, delete), edit distances between consecutive actions, output entropy, and action type distributions.\nThese features are fed to three classifiers: Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).\n\n\\subsubsection{NL Embedding Probe (ProgressProbe)}\n\\label{sec:progressprobe}\n\nEach action $a_t$ is encoded as a natural language description and embedded using Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \\citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} into a 1024-dimensional vector.\nThe $k$ embeddings within a window are processed by a 2-layer GRU \\citep{cho2014gru} with hidden size 256 and dropout 0.3.\nAn attention mechanism \\citep{bahdanau2015attention} pools the GRU outputs into a single window representation, which is passed through a fully connected layer with sigmoid activation for binary classification.\nWe also evaluate two simpler baselines: mean-pooled embeddings with logistic regression, and cosine similarity between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.\n\n\\subsubsection{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}\n\\label{sec:llm_judge}\n\nWe prompt Claude Sonnet in zero-shot mode to classify each window as stagnation or progress, given the textual descriptions of actions within the window.\nThis baseline serves exclusively for evaluation---it is not used in the silver label pipeline and incurs \\$11.66 in API cost for the SWE-Agent test set alone, making it impractical for large-scale labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Temporal Label Models}\n\\label{sec:temporal_models}\n\nThe three LFs occupy distinct temporal niches relative to stagnation onset (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}): $\\lambda_1$ leads by ${\\sim}2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ is near-concurrent, and $\\lambda_3$ lags by ${\\sim}7$ steps.\nThis motivates exploring whether encoding temporal structure into label aggregation can improve over majority vote.\n\n\\subsubsection{Time-Varying Weights (TWV)}\n\\label{sec:twv}\n\nTWV applies Gaussian kernel smoothing to each LF's activation sequence:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\tilde{\\lambda}_j(t) = \\sum_{t'} K_\\sigma(t - t') \\cdot \\lambda_j(t')\n\\label{eq:twv}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $K_\\sigma$ is a Gaussian kernel with bandwidth $\\sigma$.\nSmoothed activations replace raw votes for conf${=}1$ windows (where exactly one LF disagrees), potentially flipping labels in temporally ambiguous regions.\nThe bandwidth $\\sigma$ controls the extent of temporal context: larger values incorporate more distant steps.\n\n\\subsubsection{Hidden Markov Models}\n\\label{sec:hmm}\n\nWe model the label sequence as a Hidden Markov Model \\citep{safranchik2020linkedhmm} with two hidden states (stagnation, progress) and observations defined by the joint activation pattern of the three LFs.\nTransition and emission parameters are estimated via Expectation-Maximization (EM).\n\n\\subsubsection{Lag-Aware HMM}\n\\label{sec:lag_hmm}\n\nThe standard HMM treats all LFs as synchronous.\nLag-Aware HMM extends this by explicitly modeling each LF's temporal offset: $\\lambda_1$ is shifted by $-2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ by $-1$, and $\\lambda_3$ by $+7$, estimated from the temporal dynamics analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}).\nTo prevent divergence, we apply two regularization strategies: (1)~\\emph{confidence anchoring}, which fixes labels for conf${\\geq}$2 windows and restricts EM updates to conf${=}1$ windows; and (2)~a prior strength of 5000 on transition parameters.\nThese constraints keep the model close to the majority vote baseline while allowing it to resolve temporally ambiguous labels.\n",

1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We frame stagnation detection as window-level binary classification over agent trajectories.
5	Silver labels are generated via weak supervision from three labeling functions, then used to train and evaluate three detection approaches: structural features, a neural embedding probe, and an LLM-as-judge baseline.
6	Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} provides a system overview.
7	
8	\begin{figure*}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_pipeline.pdf}
11	\caption{System overview. (Left) Agent trajectories are segmented into overlapping windows of $k{=}5$ steps. (Center) Three labeling functions vote on each window; majority vote produces silver labels with confidence 1--3. (Right) Three detection approaches compared: structural features (30-dim), NL embedding probe (GRU), and LLM-as-judge (eval only, not trained on silver labels).}
12	\label{fig:pipeline}
13	\end{figure*}
14	
15	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
16	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
17	\label{sec:problem}
18	
19	Given an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n)$, we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$.
20	The task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \in \{0, 1\}$, where 0 denotes \emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway).
21	Window-level classification captures temporal dynamics that step-level scoring misses, while offering finer granularity than trajectory-level labeling.
22	
23	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
24	\subsection{Weak Supervision Pipeline}
25	\label{sec:weak_supervision}
26	
27	No gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.
28	We adopt a data programming approach \citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.
29	
30	\subsubsection{Labeling Functions}
31	\label{sec:labeling_functions}
32	
33	We design three LFs that target complementary signals of stagnation (Table~\ref{tab:lf_design}).
34	
35	\paragraph{Command repetition ($\lambda_1$).}
36	Computes the proportion of repeated commands within window $w_i$.
37	A high repetition ratio triggers a stagnation vote, capturing overt repetitive behavior such as re-running failing commands.
38	This LF fires on 65.6\% of SWE-Agent windows and 92.3\% of OpenHands windows; the elevated OH rate reflects differences in action distributions across agent architectures (\S\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).
39	
40	\paragraph{File diff delta ($\lambda_2$).}
41	Measures the fraction of steps within $w_i$ that produce code modifications (file creates, edits, or deletes).
42	A low modification frequency triggers a stagnation vote, detecting lack of productive output.
43	On OpenHands, this LF achieves 87\% correctness on progress windows, making it the most reliable cross-agent signal.
44	
45	\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\lambda_3$).}
46	Uses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.
47	For unresolved tasks, late-trajectory windows receive stagnation votes under the assumption that failure is preceded by unproductive behavior.
48	This LF provides retrospective confirmation rather than real-time detection.
49	
50	\begin{table}[t]
51	\centering
52	\small
53	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
54	\toprule
55	\textbf{LF} & \textbf{Signal} & \textbf{SWE Fire\,\%} & \textbf{OH Fire\,\%} \\
56	\midrule
57	$\lambda_1$ (cmd\_rep)    & Repetition & 65.6 & 92.3 \\
58	$\lambda_2$ (file\_diff)  & Output     & ---  & ---  \\
59	$\lambda_3$ (outcome\_bp) & Outcome    & ---  & ---  \\
60	\bottomrule
61	\end{tabular}
62	\caption{Labeling function design. Fire\,\% = fraction of windows on which the LF produces a stagnation vote. SWE = SWE-Agent; OH = OpenHands.}
63	\label{tab:lf_design}
64	\end{table}
65	
66	\subsubsection{Label Aggregation}
67	\label{sec:label_aggregation}
68	
69	Silver labels are produced by majority vote:
70	\begin{equation}
71	y_i = \text{MV}\bigl(\lambda_1(w_i),\; \lambda_2(w_i),\; \lambda_3(w_i)\bigr)
72	\label{eq:mv}
73	\end{equation}
74	We define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\text{conf}_i = |\{j : \lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\}| \in \{1, 2, 3\}$.
75	The conf${\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.
76	
77	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
78	\subsection{Detection Methods}
79	\label{sec:detection_methods}
80	
81	\subsubsection{Structural Features}
82	\label{sec:structural_features}
83	
84	We extract a 30-dimensional feature vector from each window, requiring no embedding model.
85	Features include: exact and fuzzy command repetition ratios, unique command ratio, file operation counts (create, modify, delete), edit distances between consecutive actions, output entropy, and action type distributions.
86	These features are fed to three classifiers: Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).
87	
88	\subsubsection{NL Embedding Probe (ProgressProbe)}
89	\label{sec:progressprobe}
90	
91	Each action $a_t$ is encoded as a natural language description and embedded using Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} into a 1024-dimensional vector.
92	The $k$ embeddings within a window are processed by a 2-layer GRU \citep{cho2014gru} with hidden size 256 and dropout 0.3.
93	An attention mechanism \citep{bahdanau2015attention} pools the GRU outputs into a single window representation, which is passed through a fully connected layer with sigmoid activation for binary classification.
94	We also evaluate two simpler baselines: mean-pooled embeddings with logistic regression, and cosine similarity between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.
95	
96	\subsubsection{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}
97	\label{sec:llm_judge}
98	
99	We prompt Claude Sonnet in zero-shot mode to classify each window as stagnation or progress, given the textual descriptions of actions within the window.
100	This baseline serves exclusively for evaluation---it is not used in the silver label pipeline and incurs \$11.66 in API cost for the SWE-Agent test set alone, making it impractical for large-scale labeling.
101	
102	%-------------------------------------------------------------------
103	\subsection{Temporal Label Models}
104	\label{sec:temporal_models}
105	
106	The three LFs occupy distinct temporal niches relative to stagnation onset (\S\ref{sec:lf_temporal}): $\lambda_1$ leads by ${\sim}2$ steps, $\lambda_2$ is near-concurrent, and $\lambda_3$ lags by ${\sim}7$ steps.
107	This motivates exploring whether encoding temporal structure into label aggregation can improve over majority vote.
108	
109	\subsubsection{Time-Varying Weights (TWV)}
110	\label{sec:twv}
111	
112	TWV applies Gaussian kernel smoothing to each LF's activation sequence:
113	\begin{equation}
114	\tilde{\lambda}_j(t) = \sum_{t'} K_\sigma(t - t') \cdot \lambda_j(t')
115	\label{eq:twv}
116	\end{equation}
117	where $K_\sigma$ is a Gaussian kernel with bandwidth $\sigma$.
118	Smoothed activations replace raw votes for conf${=}1$ windows (where exactly one LF disagrees), potentially flipping labels in temporally ambiguous regions.
119	The bandwidth $\sigma$ controls the extent of temporal context: larger values incorporate more distant steps.
120	
121	\subsubsection{Hidden Markov Models}
122	\label{sec:hmm}
123	
124	We model the label sequence as a Hidden Markov Model \citep{safranchik2020linkedhmm} with two hidden states (stagnation, progress) and observations defined by the joint activation pattern of the three LFs.
125	Transition and emission parameters are estimated via Expectation-Maximization (EM).
126	
127	\subsubsection{Lag-Aware HMM}
128	\label{sec:lag_hmm}
129	
130	The standard HMM treats all LFs as synchronous.
131	Lag-Aware HMM extends this by explicitly modeling each LF's temporal offset: $\lambda_1$ is shifted by $-2$ steps, $\lambda_2$ by $-1$, and $\lambda_3$ by $+7$, estimated from the temporal dynamics analysis (\S\ref{sec:lf_temporal}).
132	To prevent divergence, we apply two regularization strategies: (1)~\emph{confidence anchoring}, which fixes labels for conf${\geq}$2 windows and restricts EM updates to conf${=}1$ windows; and (2)~a prior strength of 5000 on transition parameters.
133	These constraints keep the model close to the majority vote baseline while allowing it to resolve temporally ambiguous labels.
134	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe frame stagnation detection as window-level binary classification over agent trajectories.\nSilver labels are generated via weak supervision from three labeling functions, then used to train and evaluate three detection approaches: structural features, a neural embedding probe, and an LLM-as-judge baseline.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} provides a system overview.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{System overview. (Left) Agent trajectories are segmented into overlapping windows of $k{=}5$ steps. (Center) Three labeling functions vote on each window; majority vote produces silver labels with confidence 1--3. (Right) Three detection approaches compared: structural features (30-dim), NL embedding probe (GRU), and LLM-as-judge (eval only, not trained on silver labels).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:problem}\n\nGiven an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_n)$, we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \\ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$.\nThe task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$, where 0 denotes \\emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \\emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway).\nWindow-level classification captures temporal dynamics that step-level scoring misses, while offering finer granularity than trajectory-level labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Weak Supervision Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:weak_supervision}\n\nNo gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nWe adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.\n\n\\subsubsection{Labeling Functions}\n\\label{sec:labeling_functions}\n\nWe design three LFs that target complementary signals of stagnation (Table~\\ref{tab:lf_design}).\n\n\\paragraph{Command repetition ($\\lambda_1$).}\nComputes the proportion of repeated commands within window $w_i$.\nA high repetition ratio triggers a stagnation vote, capturing overt repetitive behavior such as re-running failing commands.\nThis LF fires on 65.6\\% of SWE-Agent windows and 92.3\\% of OpenHands windows; the elevated OH rate reflects differences in action distributions across agent architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{File diff delta ($\\lambda_2$).}\nMeasures the fraction of steps within $w_i$ that produce code modifications (file creates, edits, or deletes).\nA low modification frequency triggers a stagnation vote, detecting lack of productive output.\nOn OpenHands, this LF achieves 87\\% correctness on progress windows, making it the most reliable cross-agent signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, late-trajectory windows receive stagnation votes under the assumption that failure is preceded by unproductive behavior.\nThis LF provides retrospective confirmation rather than real-time detection.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{LF} & \\textbf{Signal} & \\textbf{SWE Fire\\,\\%} & \\textbf{OH Fire\\,\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\lambda_1$ (cmd\\_rep)    & Repetition & 65.6 & 92.3 \\\\\n$\\lambda_2$ (file\\_diff)  & Output     & ---  & ---  \\\\\n$\\lambda_3$ (outcome\\_bp) & Outcome    & ---  & ---  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Labeling function design. Fire\\,\\% = fraction of windows on which the LF produces a stagnation vote. SWE = SWE-Agent; OH = OpenHands.}\n\\label{tab:lf_design}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsubsection{Label Aggregation}\n\\label{sec:label_aggregation}\n\nSilver labels are produced by majority vote:\n\\begin{equation}\ny_i = \\text{MV}\\bigl(\\lambda_1(w_i),\\; \\lambda_2(w_i),\\; \\lambda_3(w_i)\\bigr)\n\\label{eq:mv}\n\\end{equation}\nWe define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this   "new_string": "Given an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_n)$ where each $a_t$ is an action (e.g., a shell command, file edit, or API call), we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \\ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$ and stride 1.\nThe task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$, where 0 denotes \\emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \\emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway toward the task goal).\n\nThe window formulation offers two advantages over alternatives.\nCompared to step-level scoring, windows capture short-range temporal patterns---such as a sequence of repeated failed attempts---that individual actions cannot express.\nCompared to trajectory-level labeling, windows provide the granularity needed to pinpoint \\emph{when} stagnation begins and ends within a single task attempt, enabling downstream interventions such as early termination or strategy switching."
}

{

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513/projects/agent_progress_probe/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "No gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nWe adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.",
    "newString": "No gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nManual annotation is expensive: each window requires reviewing $k$ actions with full context (command text, outputs, file diffs), taking an estimated 2--3 minutes per window.\nWe therefore adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.\nEach LF $\\lambda_j : w_i \\to \\{0, 1\\}$ examines a window and emits a binary vote.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe frame stagnation detection as window-level binary classification over agent trajectories.\nSilver labels are generated via weak supervision from three labeling functions, then used to train and evaluate three detection approaches: structural features, a neural embedding probe, and an LLM-as-judge baseline.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} provides a system overview.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{System overview. (Left) Agent trajectories are segmented into overlapping windows of $k{=}5$ steps. (Center) Three labeling functions vote on each window; majority vote produces silver labels with confidence 1--3. (Right) Three detection approaches compared: structural features (30-dim), NL embedding probe (GRU), and LLM-as-judge (eval only, not trained on silver labels).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:problem}\n\nGiven an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_n)$ where each $a_t$ is an action (e.g., a shell command, file edit, or API call), we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \\ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$ and stride 1.\nThe task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$, where 0 denotes \\emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \\emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway toward the task goal).\n\nThe window formulation offers two advantages over alternatives.\nCompared to step-level scoring, windows capture short-range temporal patterns---such as a sequence of repeated failed attempts---that individual actions cannot express.\nCompared to trajectory-level labeling, windows provide the granularity needed to pinpoint \\emph{when} stagnation begins and ends within a single task attempt, enabling downstream interventions such as early termination or strategy switching.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Weak Supervision Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:weak_supervision}\n\nNo gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nWe adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.\n\n\\subsubsection{Labeling Functions}\n\\label{sec:labeling_functions}\n\nWe design three LFs that target complementary signals of stagnation (Table~\\ref{tab:lf_design}).\n\n\\paragraph{Command repetition ($\\lambda_1$).}\nComputes the proportion of repeated commands within window $w_i$.\nA high repetition ratio triggers a stagnation vote, capturing overt repetitive behavior such as re-running failing commands.\nThis LF fires on 65.6\\% of SWE-Agent windows and 92.3\\% of OpenHands windows; the elevated OH rate reflects differences in action distributions across agent architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{File diff delta ($\\lambda_2$).}\nMeasures the fraction of steps within $w_i$ that produce code modifications (file creates, edits, or deletes).\nA low modification frequency triggers a stagnation vote, detecting lack of productive output.\nOn OpenHands, this LF achieves 87\\% correctness on progress windows, making it the most reliable cross-agent signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, late-trajectory windows receive stagnation votes under the assumption that failure is preceded by unproductive behavior.\nThis LF provides retrospective confirmation rather than real-time detection.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{LF} & \\textbf{Signal} & \\textbf{SWE Fire\\,\\%} & \\textbf{OH Fire\\,\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\lambda_1$ (cmd\\_rep)    & Repetition & 65.6 & 92.3 \\\\\n$\\lambda_2$ (file\\_diff)  & Output     & ---  & ---  \\\\\n$\\lambda_3$ (outcome\\_bp) & Outcome    & ---  & ---  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Labeling function design. Fire\\,\\% = fraction of windows on which the LF produces a stagnation vote. SWE = SWE-Agent; OH = OpenHands.}\n\\label{tab:lf_design}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsubsection{Label Aggregation}\n\\label{sec:label_aggregation}\n\nSilver labels are produced by majority vote:\n\\begin{equation}\ny_i = \\text{MV}\\bigl(\\lambda_1(w_i),\\; \\lambda_2(w_i),\\; \\lambda_3(w_i)\\bigr)\n\\label{eq:mv}\n\\end{equation}\nWe define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Detection Methods}\n\\label{sec:detection_methods}\n\n\\subsubsection{Structural Features}\n\\label{sec:structural_features}\n\nWe extract a 30-dimensional feature vector from each window, requiring no embedding model.\nFeatures include: exact and fuzzy command repetition ratios, unique command ratio, file operation counts (create, modify, delete), edit distances between consecutive actions, output entropy, and action type distributions.\nThese features are fed to three classifiers: Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).\n\n\\subsubsection{NL Embedding Probe (ProgressProbe)}\n\\label{sec:progressprobe}\n\nEach action $a_t$ is encoded as a natural language description and embedded using Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \\citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} into a 1024-dimensional vector.\nThe $k$ embeddings within a window are processed by a 2-layer GRU \\citep{cho2014gru} with hidden size 256 and dropout 0.3.\nAn attention mechanism \\citep{bahdanau2015attention} pools the GRU outputs into a single window representation, which is passed through a fully connected layer with sigmoid activation for binary classification.\nWe also evaluate two simpler baselines: mean-pooled embeddings with logistic regression, and cosine similarity between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.\n\n\\subsubsection{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}\n\\label{sec:llm_judge}\n\nWe prompt Claude Sonnet in zero-shot mode to classify each window as stagnation or progress, given the textual descriptions of actions within the window.\nThis baseline serves exclusively for evaluation---it is not used in the silver label pipeline and incurs \\$11.66 in API cost for the SWE-Agent test set alone, making it impractical for large-scale labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Temporal Label Models}\n\\label{sec:temporal_models}\n\nThe three LFs occupy distinct temporal niches relative to stagnation onset (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}): $\\lambda_1$ leads by ${\\sim}2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ is near-concurrent, and $\\lambda_3$ lags by ${\\sim}7$ steps.\nThis motivates exploring whether encoding temporal structure into label aggregation can improve over majority vote.\n\n\\subsubsection{Time-Varying Weights (TWV)}\n\\label{sec:twv}\n\nTWV applies Gaussian kernel smoothing to each LF's activation sequence:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\tilde{\\lambda}_j(t) = \\sum_{t'} K_\\sigma(t - t') \\cdot \\lambda_j(t')\n\\label{eq:twv}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $K_\\sigma$ is a Gaussian kernel with bandwidth $\\sigma$.\nSmoothed activations replace raw votes for conf${=}1$ windows (where exactly one LF disagrees), potentially flipping labels in temporally ambiguous regions.\nThe bandwidth $\\sigma$ controls the extent of temporal context: larger values incorporate more distant steps.\n\n\\subsubsection{Hidden Markov Models}\n\\label{sec:hmm}\n\nWe model the label sequence as a Hidden Markov Model \\citep{safranchik2020linkedhmm} with two hidden states (stagnation, progress) and observations defined by the joint activation pattern of the three LFs.\nTransition and emission parameters are estimated via Expectation-Maximization (EM).\n\n\\subsubsection{Lag-Aware HMM}\n\\label{sec:lag_hmm}\n\nThe standard HMM treats all LFs as synchronous.\nLag-Aware HMM extends this by explicitly modeling each LF's temporal offset: $\\lambda_1$ is shifted by $-2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ by $-1$, and $\\lambda_3$ by $+7$, estimated from the temporal dynamics analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}).\nTo prevent divergence, we apply two regularization strategies: (1)~\\emph{confidence anchoring}, which fixes labels for conf${\\geq}$2 windows and restricts EM updates to conf${=}1$ windows; and (2)~a prior strength of 5000 on transition parameters.\nThese constraints keep the model close to the majority vote baseline while allowing it to resolve temporally ambiguous labels.\n",

          "+We therefore adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513/projects/agent_progress_probe/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, late-trajectory windows receive stagnation votes under the assumption that failure is preceded by unproductive behavior.\nThis LF provides retrospective confirmation rather than real-time detection.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, windows in the final portion of the trajectory receive stagnation votes under the assumption that task failure is preceded by a period of unproductive behavior.\nResolved tasks receive progress votes in the corresponding windows.\nUnlike $\\lambda_1$ and $\\lambda_2$, which operate on local window content, $\\lambda_3$ propagates a global signal backward through time.\nThis makes it a form of distant supervision: individually noisy, but complementary to the real-time signals when aggregated."
}

    "oldString": "We define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.",
    "newString": "We define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nWith three binary LFs, majority vote always produces a winner; conf${=}1$ arises when one LF votes opposite to the other two plus abstains differently, producing the most ambiguous labels.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree on the winning label, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.\nThis stratification allows us to separately analyze model behavior on high-confidence vs.\\ ambiguous windows (\\S\\ref{sec:main_results}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe frame stagnation detection as window-level binary classification over agent trajectories.\nSilver labels are generated via weak supervision from three labeling functions, then used to train and evaluate three detection approaches: structural features, a neural embedding probe, and an LLM-as-judge baseline.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} provides a system overview.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{System overview. (Left) Agent trajectories are segmented into overlapping windows of $k{=}5$ steps. (Center) Three labeling functions vote on each window; majority vote produces silver labels with confidence 1--3. (Right) Three detection approaches compared: structural features (30-dim), NL embedding probe (GRU), and LLM-as-judge (eval only, not trained on silver labels).}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:problem}\n\nGiven an agent trajectory $T = (a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_n)$ where each $a_t$ is an action (e.g., a shell command, file edit, or API call), we partition it into overlapping sliding windows $w_i = (a_i, \\ldots, a_{i+k-1})$ with window size $k{=}5$ and stride 1.\nThe task is to assign each window a binary label $y_i \\in \\{0, 1\\}$, where 0 denotes \\emph{stagnation} (the agent is stuck or unproductive) and 1 denotes \\emph{progress} (the agent is making meaningful headway toward the task goal).\n\nThe window formulation offers two advantages over alternatives.\nCompared to step-level scoring, windows capture short-range temporal patterns---such as a sequence of repeated failed attempts---that individual actions cannot express.\nCompared to trajectory-level labeling, windows provide the granularity needed to pinpoint \\emph{when} stagnation begins and ends within a single task attempt, enabling downstream interventions such as early termination or strategy switching.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Weak Supervision Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:weak_supervision}\n\nNo gold-standard annotations exist for agent stagnation at the window level.\nManual annotation is expensive: each window requires reviewing $k$ actions with full context (command text, outputs, file diffs), taking an estimated 2--3 minutes per window.\nWe therefore adopt a data programming approach \\citep{ratner2016dataprogramming,ratner2017snorkel}: multiple labeling functions (LFs) provide noisy votes that are aggregated into silver labels.\nEach LF $\\lambda_j : w_i \\to \\{0, 1\\}$ examines a window and emits a binary vote.\n\n\\subsubsection{Labeling Functions}\n\\label{sec:labeling_functions}\n\nWe design three LFs that target complementary signals of stagnation (Table~\\ref{tab:lf_design}).\n\n\\paragraph{Command repetition ($\\lambda_1$).}\nComputes the proportion of repeated commands within window $w_i$.\nA high repetition ratio triggers a stagnation vote, capturing overt repetitive behavior such as re-running failing commands.\nThis LF fires on 65.6\\% of SWE-Agent windows and 92.3\\% of OpenHands windows; the elevated OH rate reflects differences in action distributions across agent architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:cmd_rep_ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{File diff delta ($\\lambda_2$).}\nMeasures the fraction of steps within $w_i$ that produce code modifications (file creates, edits, or deletes).\nA low modification frequency triggers a stagnation vote, detecting lack of productive output.\nOn OpenHands, this LF achieves 87\\% correctness on progress windows, making it the most reliable cross-agent signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Outcome backpropagation ($\\lambda_3$).}\nUses the final task outcome (resolved/unresolved) as distant supervision.\nFor unresolved tasks, windows in the final portion of the trajectory receive stagnation votes under the assumption that task failure is preceded by a period of unproductive behavior.\nResolved tasks receive progress votes in the corresponding windows.\nUnlike $\\lambda_1$ and $\\lambda_2$, which operate on local window content, $\\lambda_3$ propagates a global signal backward through time.\nThis makes it a form of distant supervision: individually noisy, but complementary to the real-time signals when aggregated.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{LF} & \\textbf{Signal} & \\textbf{SWE Fire\\,\\%} & \\textbf{OH Fire\\,\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\lambda_1$ (cmd\\_rep)    & Repetition & 65.6 & 92.3 \\\\\n$\\lambda_2$ (file\\_diff)  & Output     & ---  & ---  \\\\\n$\\lambda_3$ (outcome\\_bp) & Outcome    & ---  & ---  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Labeling function design. Fire\\,\\% = fraction of windows on which the LF produces a stagnation vote. SWE = SWE-Agent; OH = OpenHands.}\n\\label{tab:lf_design}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsubsection{Label Aggregation}\n\\label{sec:label_aggregation}\n\nSilver labels are produced by majority vote:\n\\begin{equation}\ny_i = \\text{MV}\\bigl(\\lambda_1(w_i),\\; \\lambda_2(w_i),\\; \\lambda_3(w_i)\\bigr)\n\\label{eq:mv}\n\\end{equation}\nWe define the confidence of each label as the number of agreeing LFs: $\\text{conf}_i = |\\{j : \\lambda_j(w_i) = y_i\\}| \\in \\{1, 2, 3\\}$.\nThe conf${\\geq}$2 subset, where at least two LFs agree, serves as the primary evaluation set throughout this work.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Detection Methods}\n\\label{sec:detection_methods}\n\n\\subsubsection{Structural Features}\n\\label{sec:structural_features}\n\nWe extract a 30-dimensional feature vector from each window, requiring no embedding model.\nFeatures include: exact and fuzzy command repetition ratios, unique command ratio, file operation counts (create, modify, delete), edit distances between consecutive actions, output entropy, and action type distributions.\nThese features are fed to three classifiers: Logistic Regression (LR), Random Forest (RF), and Gradient Boosting (GB).\n\n\\subsubsection{NL Embedding Probe (ProgressProbe)}\n\\label{sec:progressprobe}\n\nEach action $a_t$ is encoded as a natural language description and embedded using Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B \\citep{zhang2025qwen3embedding} into a 1024-dimensional vector.\nThe $k$ embeddings within a window are processed by a 2-layer GRU \\citep{cho2014gru} with hidden size 256 and dropout 0.3.\nAn attention mechanism \\citep{bahdanau2015attention} pools the GRU outputs into a single window representation, which is passed through a fully connected layer with sigmoid activation for binary classification.\nWe also evaluate two simpler baselines: mean-pooled embeddings with logistic regression, and cosine similarity between consecutive window embeddings with a validation-tuned threshold.\n\n\\subsubsection{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}\n\\label{sec:llm_judge}\n\nWe prompt Claude Sonnet in zero-shot mode to classify each window as stagnation or progress, given the textual descriptions of actions within the window.\nThis baseline serves exclusively for evaluation---it is not used in the silver label pipeline and incurs \\$11.66 in API cost for the SWE-Agent test set alone, making it impractical for large-scale labeling.\n\n%-------------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Temporal Label Models}\n\\label{sec:temporal_models}\n\nThe three LFs occupy distinct temporal niches relative to stagnation onset (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}): $\\lambda_1$ leads by ${\\sim}2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ is near-concurrent, and $\\lambda_3$ lags by ${\\sim}7$ steps.\nThis motivates exploring whether encoding temporal structure into label aggregation can improve over majority vote.\n\n\\subsubsection{Time-Varying Weights (TWV)}\n\\label{sec:twv}\n\nTWV applies Gaussian kernel smoothing to each LF's activation sequence:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\tilde{\\lambda}_j(t) = \\sum_{t'} K_\\sigma(t - t') \\cdot \\lambda_j(t')\n\\label{eq:twv}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $K_\\sigma$ is a Gaussian kernel with bandwidth $\\sigma$.\nSmoothed activations replace raw votes for conf${=}1$ windows (where exactly one LF disagrees), potentially flipping labels in temporally ambiguous regions.\nThe bandwidth $\\sigma$ controls the extent of temporal context: larger values incorporate more distant steps.\n\n\\subsubsection{Hidden Markov Models}\n\\label{sec:hmm}\n\nWe model the label sequence as a Hidden Markov Model \\citep{safranchik2020linkedhmm} with two hidden states (stagnation, progress) and observations defined by the joint activation pattern of the three LFs.\nTransition and emission parameters are estimated via Expectation-Maximization (EM).\n\n\\subsubsection{Lag-Aware HMM}\n\\label{sec:lag_hmm}\n\nThe standard HMM treats all LFs as synchronous.\nLag-Aware HMM extends this by explicitly modeling each LF's temporal offset: $\\lambda_1$ is shifted by $-2$ steps, $\\lambda_2$ by $-1$, and $\\lambda_3$ by $+7$, estimated from the temporal dynamics analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:lf_temporal}).\nTo prevent divergence, we apply two regularization strategies: (1)~\\emph{confidence anchoring}, which fixes labels for conf${\\geq}$2 windows and restricts EM updates to conf${=}1$ windows; and (2)~a prior strength of 5000 on transition parameters.\nThese constraints keep the model close to the majority vote baseline while allowing it to resolve temporally ambiguous labels.\n",
